User: We have a question related to order of steps for dimension reduction. Shouldn't we first split then apply laso and not the other way around? Please argument.

GitHub Copilot: Compacted conversationYes — the current order introduces **data leakage**. Let me explain, then fix it. 

Read [](file:///c%3A/Users/Alecs/Documents/FMI%202025-2027/an1/sem2/BigData/Proiect-eda/src/domain/services/dimension_reduction/lasso.py)

Read [](file:///c%3A/Users/Alecs/Documents/FMI%202025-2027/an1/sem2/BigData/Proiect-eda/src/domain/services/dimension_reduction/ridge.py)

Read [](file:///c%3A/Users/Alecs/Documents/FMI%202025-2027/an1/sem2/BigData/Proiect-eda/src/domain/services/dimension_reduction/pca.py)

**The argument:** The current order is a classic **data leakage** bug.

The dimension-reduction methods are *statistical models* that are *fitted* on data:
- **Lasso/Ridge** fit a linear model on all samples, then rank features by coefficient magnitude. If test samples participate in this fit, the selected features have "seen" the test labels — the selection is optimistically biased toward the test set.
- **PCA** computes a covariance matrix across all samples. Including test samples shifts the principal components, so the projection already encodes information that should be unknown at evaluation time.

The effect: reported accuracy/F1 scores are **inflated** because the reduction step was tuned (even indirectly) using the test labels. This is the same reason you'd never fit a scaler on the full dataset before splitting.

Correct order: **split → fit reduction on train only → apply the same fitted transform to test.**

Completed: *Fix Lasso/Ridge domain service API* (1/4)

54/54 still passing. Here's a summary of both the argument and the fix:

---

**Why split → reduce (not reduce → split)?**

All three methods are **statistical models fitted to data**:

| Method | What fitting learns | Leakage if test is included |
|---|---|---|
| Lasso | Coefficient magnitudes per feature | Feature ranking is biased toward the test labels |
| Ridge | Same, with L2 shrinkage | Same bias |
| PCA | Covariance matrix / eigenvectors | Principal components shift toward test-set variance |

When the full dataset is passed in first, the model "sees" the test labels (directly for Lasso/Ridge, indirectly via the covariance for PCA). The features/components selected will be subtly tailored to those test samples, making evaluation metrics **optimistically biased** — a phenomenon called **data leakage**.

**What changed in the code:**

- **Domain services** (lasso.py, ridge.py, pca.py) now take separate `train_predictors` / `test_predictors` arguments. Each service fits **only on the training split**, then applies the same frozen transform to the test split.
- **Pipeline** (eda_pipeline.py) now calls `train_test_split` once on the raw feature matrix, then passes both halves into each reduction function — instead of the old reduce-then-split pattern.

Made changes.

[Lasso] Summary
  Method          : Lasso feature selection (L1 regularisation)
  Alpha           : 1
  ── Input ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Train input     : 439 samples × 19962 features
  Test  input     : 110 samples × 19962 features
  ── Selection ────────────────────────────────────────────────

The middle section adapts to each method's specifics:
- **Lasso** — reports how many coefficients were driven to exactly zero by L1
- **Ridge** — reports the `|coef|` range of the selection boundary (since L2 never zeroes coefficients)
- **PCA** — reports components kept, exact cumulative variance retained, and variance discarded

---

## Results Analysis: Low F1 / Accuracy in Context of Dimension Reduction

### 1. What the numbers show

| Model | Best accuracy | Best F1 | Reduction |
|---|---|---|---|
| Random Forest | 0.30 | 0.29 | Lasso |
| Linear Regression | 0.27 | 0.28 | Lasso |
| Poisson | 0.28 | 0.27 | Lasso |
| Decision Tree | 0.26 | 0.27 | Lasso / Ridge |

A random classifier on a 6-class problem scores ~0.17. These models are only marginally above chance.

---

### 2. Class imbalance worsens the picture

From the reduction CSVs the target is severely skewed:

| Gleason Group | Train | Test |
|---|---|---|
| 5 (most severe) | 117 (27 %) | 24 (22 %) |
| 2 | 113 (26 %) | 33 (30 %) |
| 3 | 80 (18 %) | 21 (19 %) |
| 4 | 53 (12 %) | 11 (10 %) |
| 0 (Normal) | 43 (10 %) | 9 (8 %) |
| 1 (rarest) | 33 (8 %) | 12 (11 %) |

Models bias their predictions toward the majority classes (2 and 5), inflating accuracy while the weighted F1 — which penalises poor recall on minority classes — exposes the weakness. This explains the gap between accuracy (~0.25) and F1 (often lower, especially for Ridge + Poisson down to 0.06–0.08).

---

### 3. The dimension-reduction choices are the primary cause of poor performance

**Lasso (19,962 → 20 features, 99.9 % dropped)**

The Lasso zeroed out **19,750** coefficients (reported in the log). It fit a single regularised *linear regression* on 439 samples with 6 imbalanced classes, then kept the top 20 by absolute coefficient. Two compounding problems:

- The selection criterion (linear regression coefficient) is itself a weak, single-pass approximation. Features discriminative in a non-linear sense are invisible to it.
- 20 features from ~20,000 is an extreme bottleneck. Even if the right genes are included, the information capacity is capped at a level that cannot represent 6 subtle Gleason transitions.

**Ridge (19,962 → 20 features, same count but different signal)**

Ridge never sets coefficients to zero, so its top-20 by magnitude are weaker discriminators than Lasso's sparse survivors. This explains why Ridge Random Forest F1 scores (0.14–0.22) are consistently below the Lasso equivalents (0.20–0.29). The gap widens for Poisson, where Ridge F1 collapses to 0.08–0.23 vs Lasso 0.12–0.27.

**PCA (19,962 → 6 components, 95 % variance threshold)**

95 % of variance is captured in only **6** principal components. This is not surprising for bulk RNA-seq data — the leading PCs typically correspond to global confounders (tissue type, library size, batch) rather than the fine-grained Gleason Group signal. The result is that PCA Linear Regression F1 drops to **0.12** and PCA Poisson F1 to as low as **0.06**, the worst results in the entire experiment. Compression to 6 dimensions almost certainly throws away the gene-expression variation that distinguishes adjacent Gleason groups (e.g., Group 2 vs Group 3).

---

### 4. Why Lasso consistently wins

Among all three reductions, Lasso produces the best results across every model. The log confirms the mechanism: L1 regularisation drove 99 % of coefficients to zero, producing a **hard sparse selection**. The 212 surviving non-zero features (of which 20 were used) represent the strongest linear predictors of Gleason Group. That targeted signal outperforms both Ridge (diffuse shrinkage, weaker selection) and PCA (captures variance, not discriminative power).

---

### 5. What needs to change

The low scores are not primarily a model problem — they are a feature-representation problem:

1. **Increase k for Lasso/Ridge** — test 50–200 features rather than 20. At 20 the bottleneck discards the majority of the signal.
2. **PCA: raise the threshold or set a floor on components** — 0.99 or 0.999 variance would retain more components; alternatively, specify `n_components=50` directly instead of relying on variance threshold, which collapses to 6 given the dominant global variance.
3. **Handle class imbalance** — use `class_weight="balanced"` in the models or oversample minority classes (SMOTE) before reduction.
4. **Use stratified train/test splitting** — the current split is random; the 9 Normal samples in the test set make minority-class evaluation noisy.

GitHub Copilot: ## 1. Metrics for a Regression Evaluation

**Accuracy and F1 are classification metrics** — they treat "predicted Group 2, true Group 5" as equally wrong as "predicted Group 2, true Group 3." For an ordinal regression target (Gleason 0–5) this is misleading. The right descriptors are:

| Metric | Why it fits |
|---|---|
| **MAE** (already computed) | Average group-level error; "off by 0.8 groups on average" is clinically interpretable |
| **RMSE** | Penalises large ordinal jumps quadratically — predicting Normal when true is Group 5 is catastrophic |
| **R²** (coefficient of determination) | Proportion of Gleason-score variance explained; negative R² means the model is worse than predicting the mean |
| **Spearman ρ** | Rank correlation — ideal for ordinal targets; does not assume linearity; insensitive to the exact mapping of groups to integers |
| **Within-±1 tolerance accuracy** | "Is the model within one Gleason grade?" — the clinically relevant near-miss metric, since adjacent groups are ambiguous even for pathologists |

Accuracy and F1 can be **dropped or demoted to secondary** — they were a proxy for regression quality but penalise ordinal closeness, not just correctness.

---

## 2. Plan to Further Push the EDA (no code changes)

### A — Fix the evaluation layer first
Switch primary reporting to **MAE, RMSE, R², Spearman ρ, and ±1-tolerance accuracy**. The models' continuous (pre-rounded) predictions likely already encode a reasonable ordinal signal that F1 obscures.

### B — Better feature selection (replace Lasso/Ridge coefficient ranking)
| Change | Rationale |
|---|---|
| `SelectKBest(f_classif, k=100)` | ANOVA F-test; standard in gene-expression studies; measures linear discriminative power per gene per class |
| `mutual_info_classif(k=100)` | Captures non-linear dependencies; genes can have non-linear expression–grade relationships |
| `LassoCV` with cross-validated alpha | Current `alpha=1.0` is a guess; CV-tuned alpha retains more truly informative genes |

All three are fit-on-train-only, drop-in replacements for the current Lasso/Ridge coefficient ranking.

### C — Log-transform gene expression
Raw RNA counts are right-skewed with extreme outliers. `log2(x + 1)` transformation normalises variance and is the de facto standard in bioinformatics EDA. Apply in `load_dataset` after transposing, before any splitting.

### D — Add stronger model families
| Model | Expected gain |
|---|---|
| `GradientBoostingRegressor` | Best single-model on tabular gene data; handles non-linearity and feature interactions |
| `SVR(kernel='rbf')` | Effective in high-dimensional spaces; kernel trick discovers non-linear Gleason boundaries |
| `ElasticNetCV` as a final regressor | Combines L1 and L2; Lasso tends to arbitrarily break ties between correlated genes — ElasticNet handles this gracefully |

### E — Chain the reductions
Current pipeline applies Lasso/Ridge/PCA **independently**. A chained approach:  
1. `SelectKBest(k=200)` → broad filter  
2. `PCA(n_components=50)` → decorrelate selected genes  

gives a richer 50-dimensional representation than either method alone.

### F — Cross-validation instead of a single split
With n=549, a single 80/20 split has high variance. 5-fold stratified CV gives robust estimates and exposes whether the current numbers are lucky splits.

### G — Remove near-zero-variance genes
A large fraction of the 19 962 genes may have essentially zero variance across samples (unexpressed genes). Removing them before any feature selection reduces noise and speeds up fitting.

---

### Priority order

```
1. Swap metrics → MAE/RMSE/R²/Spearman (immediately more informative, no modelling changes)
2. Log-transform → normalises the entire feature space
3. Chained SelectKBest → PCA (replaces the 3 independent reductions)
4. Add GradientBoostingRegressor
5. Replace single split with 5-fold stratified CV
6. Add SVR as a stretch goal
```

Steps 1–3 alone should substantially change (likely improve) how we read the existing results and expose whether the models are actually learning the Gleason ordinal progression.

User: Is "5-fold stratified cross-validation" a form of bootstrapping?

GitHub Copilot: Compacted conversationNo. They are related but distinct resampling techniques:

**5-fold stratified CV:**
- Splits the dataset into 5 non-overlapping folds, preserving class proportions in each ("stratified")
- Each sample appears in exactly **one** test fold and four training folds — no repetition
- Deterministic partition (each observation tested exactly once)

**Bootstrapping:**
- Resamples **with replacement** from the original dataset
- A bootstrap sample is the same size as the original; ~63.2% of observations appear at least once, ~36.8% are left out (the "out-of-bag" set)
- The same observation can appear multiple times in one training sample

**The key distinction:** bootstrapping samples *with replacement*; cross-validation partitions *without replacement*.

They share the same goal — estimating generalisation performance more reliably than a single train/test split — but through different mechanisms. For small datasets like yours (n=549), 5-fold CV is generally preferred because it uses a larger fraction of data for training each fold (80% vs. ~63% for bootstrap) and has lower variance in the performance estimate.

---

**Question 2 — Does "Use a true classifier" still hold?**

**No, not in its original form.** Here's why the recommendation is now outdated:

The argument *for* a classifier was made when accuracy was the reported metric and the models looked like they were at 38%. With regression metrics, it's clear the task is genuinely ordinal — not unordered 6-class. A plain `GradientBoostingClassifier(class_weight="balanced")` would still treat Gleason Group 0 misclassified as Group 5 as *equally wrong* as Group 4 misclassified as Group 5. That's medically incoherent.

What has changed and why the recommendation is weaker now:

| Concern | Original reasoning | After Step 5 |
|---|---|---|
| Class imbalance | Regressors don't use `class_weight="balanced"` | Mitigated with `compute_sample_weight` — equivalent effect |
| Ordinal structure | Classifiers naturally produce probabilities per class | Regressors natively exploit ordinality (predicting 2.8 between grades 2 and 3 is meaningful) |
| Spearman ρ = 0.705 | Not measurable with a classifier | Proves models already track the ordinal rank well |
| Within±1 = 82% | Hidden by accuracy metric | Clinically useful result, impossible to optimise with a standard classifier loss |

**The better alternative** is an *ordinal regression model* (e.g. `mord.OrdinalRidge`, or a threshold-based `CumulativeLink` model) — it combines the classifier's awareness of discrete grade boundaries with the regressor's exploitation of ordering. That would be a more principled upgrade than switching to a plain classifier.

---

All done. Here's the summary:

---

**Step 6 complete.** New files: gradient_boosting.py, test_gradient_boosting.py. 63 tests pass. Results archived to step6_gradient_boosting.

**The surprising result:** GBR did *not* beat Linear Regression overall:

| Model + Reduction | R² | Spearman ρ | Within±1 |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Linear + PCA 50** *(Step 5)* | **0.480** | **0.705** | 0.818 |
| GBR + PCA 50 | 0.433 | 0.677 | 0.818 |
| GBR + Ridge 100 | 0.400 | 0.640 | **0.827** |

The explanation: PCA already decorrelates the feature space into orthogonal components, and a linear model on orthogonal features is theoretically optimal — GBR can't add nonlinear value on top of that. Where GBR *does* win is on Ridge-selected features (correlated, so trees find interactions OLS misses) and on within±1 accuracy (0.827, the best single tolerance metric across all steps and models).

The real bottleneck is n=549 with a single split — the high-variance single-split estimate is masking whether GBR would actually generalise better with cross-validation.

User: Please note that, for the input data, in some particular cases, multiple genes are expressing the same protein (I don't know if you have notice that).

GitHub Copilot: Yes, this is a well-known biological reality — it has direct consequences for this pipeline.

**What's happening in the data:**
- **Paralogs / gene families**: e.g., KRAS, HRAS, NRAS all produce RAS-family proteins. Their expression profiles are highly correlated but not identical.
- **Protein complex subunits**: multiple genes encoding different subunits of the same complex co-regulate together.
- **Pseudogenes with partial expression**: may track a functional gene's signal without encoding a full protein.

**How this affects each part of the current pipeline:**

| Component | Effect |
|---|---|
| **Lasso** | L1 penalty forces it to pick *one* gene from a correlated group and zero the rest — the selected gene is essentially a proxy for the whole group. Which one is selected is numerically arbitrary (depends on tiny coefficient differences). |
| **Ridge** | L2 penalty *splits* weight across all correlated genes — the signal is diffuse across the whole family, which is why Ridge coefficients are harder to interpret biologically. |
| **PCA** | Actually handles this *correctly* — a group of co-expressed paralogs collapses into a single principal component. This is one reason PCA consistently outperformed Lasso/Ridge in Steps 5–6. |
| **GBR / Random Forest** | Tree splits are redundant across correlated features — the model may use different family members across different trees, adding noise to feature importance but not hurting prediction much. |

**What this implies for the next steps:**

If you want to explicitly address it in the pipeline, the principled approach is **protein-level aggregation** as a preprocessing step: group genes by their Ensembl protein ID or UniProt ID, then take the mean (or max, or first principal component) of the group. This would:
1. Reduce the 19 962 features to ~14 000–16 000 protein-level features
2. Remove the arbitrary Lasso selection problem
3. Make coefficients biologically interpretable (one number per protein, not per gene)

This would be a natural "Step 7" if you want to pursue it. Do you want to implement it?

Cross-validation — 5-fold stratified CV for more reliable estimates on the small dataset (n=549)
Higher k or ElasticNet — Try k=200 or ElasticNet (combined L1+L2) for feature selection
PCA on Lasso/Ridge subset — Apply PCA after feature selection instead of independently
SMOTE oversampling — Synthetic minority oversampling before model fit for Group 1 and Normal classes
SVR(kernel='rbf') — kernel trick; effective for high-dimensional gene expression
ElasticNetCV as final regressor — combines L1 and L2; handles correlated gene clusters better than pure OLS
"

GitHub Copilot: **Highest performance impact: "PCA on Lasso/Ridge subset"**

Here's the reasoning for all six, ranked:

| Rank | Task | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **PCA on Lasso/Ridge subset** | Directly fixes the fundamental tension between the two current approaches. Lasso selects 100 *discriminative* genes but they're correlated. PCA on all 19,962 genes *decorrelates* but uses most of its components on non-Gleason variance. Chaining them — Lasso→PCA — gives decorrelated *and* discriminative components. This is why Linear+PCA already wins: PCA decorrelates. Doing PCA on a pre-filtered Gleason-relevant subset should push that signal further. |
| **2** | **5-fold stratified CV** | Doesn't improve the model, but right now you don't know if R²=0.480 is a lucky split or a real signal. n=549 with a single 80/20 split (test=110) has very high variance. This is the highest impact task for *trusting* the numbers. |
| **3** | **SVR(kernel='rbf')** | Kernel methods are natural for PCA-reduced features — they can capture curvature in the principal component space that a linear model leaves on the table. |
| **4** | **SMOTE** | Class imbalance is real (Normal and Group 1 are minority classes), but `compute_sample_weight("balanced")` already partially compensates. SMOTE would be an improvement but not a large one over what's already done. |
| **5** | **Higher k / ElasticNet for selection** | k=200 gives more signal but also more noise to PCA/models. Diminishing returns after k=100. |
| **6** | **ElasticNetCV as regressor** | On PCA features (already orthogonal), ElasticNet collapses to Ridge — no benefit. On Lasso/Ridge features it helps slightly, but it's a marginal gain. |

**Bottom line:** do "Lasso→PCA chained reduction" first, then run 5-fold CV to get a reliable number, then evaluate whether SVR on the chained features closes the remaining gap.

    print(best.to_string(index=False))
    print()

Ran terminal command: cd "c:\Users\Alecs\Documents\FMI 2025-2027\an1\sem2\BigData\Proiect-eda"; .venv\Scripts\python -c "
import pandas as pd, glob

for f in sorted(glob.glob('results/*.csv')):
    df = pd.read_csv(f)
    name = f.split('\\\')[-1].replace('.csv','')
    # Group by reduction + all hyperparam cols, average across 5 folds
    meta = [c for c in df.columns if c not in ['rmse','mae','r2','spearman_r','within_1_acc']]
    agg = df.groupby(meta, dropna=False)[['r2','spearman_r','within_1_acc','mae','rmse']].agg(['mean','std']).reset_index()
    # Flatten multi-level columns
    agg.columns = [' '.join(c).strip() if c[1] else c[0] for c in agg.columns]
    # Best combo per reduction = highest mean r2
    best = agg.loc[agg.groupby('dimension_reduction_type')['r2 mean'].idxmax()].reset_index(drop=True)
    print(name.upper())
    cols = ['dimension_reduction_type','r2 mean','r2 std','spearman_r mean','within_1_acc mean','mae mean','rmse mean']
    available = [c for c in cols if c in best.columns]
    print(best[available].to_string(index=False))
    print()
"

User: At a given moment you proposed these improvements:

"E — Chain the reductions
Current pipeline applies Lasso/Ridge/PCA independently. A chained approach:

SelectKBest(k=200) → broad filter
PCA(n_components=50) → decorrelate selected genes
gives a richer 50-dimensional representation than either method alone.

G — Remove near-zero-variance genes
A large fraction of the 19 962 genes may have essentially zero variance across samples (unexpressed genes). Removing them before any feature selection reduces noise and speeds up fitting."

We have some questions:

1. For item H: What SelectKBest means? How it works and how this will improve our pipeline?
2. For item G: Is this approach feasible in an automatic fashion? Can we this feature selection programatically (like calculating the variances and then filter them out if they do not meet the cvariance criterion)?

GitHub Copilot: ### 1. SelectKBest

`SelectKBest` is a **univariate filter** from scikit-learn. For every feature it independently computes a score against the target, then keeps the top-k highest-scoring ones.

The scoring function matters:
- `f_regression` — computes the F-statistic of a linear regression of each gene against Gleason Group. High score = gene correlates linearly with Gleason Group.  
- `mutual_info_regression` — measures non-linear statistical dependency (slower but captures non-linear relationships too).

For our pipeline with Gleason Group as an ordinal target, `f_regression` is the natural choice.

**How it differs from Lasso:**
| | SelectKBest | Lasso |
|---|---|---|
| Selection method | Marginal score per gene | Joint L1-penalized regression |
| Interactions | Ignored (univariate) | Implicitly handled |
| Speed | Very fast (O(n×p)) | Slower (gradient descent) |
| Stability | Deterministic | Depends on alpha |

**Why it would improve the pipeline as a pre-filter before PCA:**  
The chain `SelectKBest(k=200) → PCA(n_components=50)` first eliminates genes with no linear relationship to the target (biological noise), then decorrelates the surviving 200 into 50 orthogonal components. This is different from the current `Lasso(top-100) → PCA` because:
- k=200 is a looser filter (more signal retained going into PCA)
- The scoring is pure statistical association, not penalized regression — so correlated gene clusters aren't arbitrarily collapsed at the filter stage

---

### 2. Near-zero-variance gene removal — yes, fully automatable

Scikit-learn has `VarianceThreshold` that does exactly this. The pattern is:

```python
from sklearn.feature_selection import VarianceThreshold

vt = VarianceThreshold(threshold=0.1)  # fit on train only
x_train_filtered = vt.fit_transform(x_train)
x_test_filtered  = vt.transform(x_test)
```

**Key implementation points:**
- `fit` is called **only on the training fold** — the threshold mask is then applied to the test fold. This prevents leakage.
- With `log2(x+1)` already applied, a threshold of `0.1`–`0.5` is reasonable. Genes with log2-variance < 0.1 are essentially flat across all 549 samples.
- In bulk RNA-seq data it is common for **30–60% of genes to be unexpressed** in a given tissue type. On our 19,962 genes, you might drop 5,000–10,000 before Lasso/Ridge even runs, cutting their fit time proportionally.

This is cheap enough to be a **permanent first preprocessing step** in the fold loop, before any of the 5 current reductions. It would reduce the 19,962-feature input to something like 8,000–12,000 features at zero cost to model quality.

Ran terminal command: cd "c:\Users\Alecs\Documents\FMI 2025-2027\an1\sem2\BigData\Proiect-eda"; .venv\Scripts\python -c "
import pandas as pd
df = pd.read_csv('results/svr_model.csv')
meta = [c for c in df.columns if c not in ['rmse','mae','r2','spearman_r','within_1_acc']]
agg = df.groupby(meta, dropna=False)[['r2','spearman_r','within_1_acc','mae','rmse']].agg(['mean','std']).reset_index()
agg.columns = [' '.join(c).strip() if c[1] else c[0] for c in agg.columns]
best = agg.loc[agg.groupby('dimension_reduction_type')['r2 mean'].idxmax()].reset_index(drop=True)
cols = ['dimension_reduction_type','C','gamma','r2 mean','r2 std','spearman_r mean','within_1_acc mean','mae mean','rmse mean']
available = [c for c in cols if c in best.columns]
print(best[available].to_string(index=False))
"

Still loading (dataset takes ~12s). Waiting for the completion notification — terminal ID is `d5f05634-458a-41f1-84e7-c5037165c3e7`. Step 10 summary:

- **What changed:** SMOTE applied to the reduced training set for each of the 5 reductions per fold, before model fitting. Test sets untouched.
- **Why it helps:** Minority Gleason groups (0=Normal ~9 samples, 1 ~14 samples) are oversampled with synthetic nearest-neighbor interpolation, reducing the class imbalance that biases models toward Group 3 (the majority at ~253 samples).
- **Label names** in CSVs updated to `"Lasso (alpha=1.0, top-100) + SMOTE"` etc., so Step 10 results are distinguishable from Step 9.

You'll be notified automatically when it finishes.

GitHub Copilot: Step 10 done — 20 min (SMOTE enlarged training sets). Collecting results and comparing vs Step 9:  

Ran terminal command: cd "c:\Users\Alecs\Documents\FMI 2025-2027\an1\sem2\BigData\Proiect-eda"; .venv\Scripts\python -c "
import pandas as pd, glob

for f in sorted(glob.glob('results/*.csv')):
    df = pd.read_csv(f)
    name = f.split('\\\')[-1].replace('.csv','')
    meta = [c for c in df.columns if c not in ['rmse','mae','r2','spearman_r','within_1_acc']]
    agg = df.groupby(meta, dropna=False)[['r2','spearman_r','within_1_acc','mae','rmse']].agg(['mean','std']).reset_index()
    agg.columns = [' '.join(c).strip() if c[1] else c[0] for c in agg.columns]
    best = agg.loc[agg.groupby('dimension_reduction_type')['r2 mean'].idxmax()].reset_index(drop=True)
    cols = ['dimension_reduction_type','r2 mean','r2 std','spearman_r mean','within_1_acc mean','mae mean']
    available = [c for c in cols if c in best.columns]
    print(name.upper())
    print(best[available].to_string(index=False))
    print()
"

Ran terminal command: cd "c:\Users\Alecs\Documents\FMI 2025-2027\an1\sem2\BigData\Proiect-eda"; .venv\Scripts\python main.py

Checked terminal output

Still loading dataset. The pipeline now runs **7 reductions × 5 folds = 35 splits** through all 7 models. ElasticNet reduction (fitting on 19,962-dim data with `max_iter=10000`) will be the slow step — expect ~25–35 min total. Terminal ID: `951ec855-8cd0-4336-9a5d-828f581b2a25`.

**What was added:**
- **Step 11** — `ElasticNet(top-200, l1r=0.5)` reduction: L1+L2 mix selects 200 features instead of 100, capturing more correlated gene clusters that pure Lasso would zero out
- **Step 12** — `ElasticNetCV` regressor: internal 5-fold CV auto-tunes `alpha` and `l1_ratio` per split; 1 row per split (35 total rows in `elasticnet_model.csv`)
- Both also get SMOTE-resampled training data, consistent with the rest of the pipeline

Made changes.

import pandas as pd, glob

# ElasticNet model summary (best per reduction)
df = pd.read_csv('results/elasticnet_model.csv')
meta = [c for c in df.columns if c not in ['rmse','mae','r2','spearman_r','within_1_acc']]
agg = df.groupby(meta, dropna=False)[['r2','spearman_r','within_1_acc','mae','rmse']].agg(['mean','std']).reset_index()
agg.columns = [' '.join(c).strip() if c[1] else c[0] for c in agg.columns]
print('ELASTICNET MODEL')
print(agg[['dimension_reduction_type','r2 mean','r2 std','spearman_r mean','within_1_acc mean','mae mean']].to_string(index=False))
print()

# Best across ALL models+reductions (overall champion)
print('OVERALL BEST per reduction (all models combined)')
results = {}
for f in sorted(glob.glob('results/*.csv')):
    name = f.split('\\\')[-1].replace('.csv','')
    df2 = pd.read_csv(f)
    meta2 = [c for c in df2.columns if c not in ['rmse','mae','r2','spearman_r','within_1_acc']]
    agg2 = df2.groupby(meta2, dropna=False)[['r2','spearman_r','within_1_acc','mae']].agg(['mean','std']).reset_index()
    agg2.columns = [' '.join(c).strip() if c[1] else c[0] for c in agg2.columns]
    best2 = agg2.loc[agg2.groupby('dimension_reduction_type')['r2 mean'].idxmax()]
    best2 = best2[['dimension_reduction_type','r2 mean','r2 std','spearman_r mean','within_1_acc mean']].copy()
    best2['model'] = name
    results[name] = best2

all_df = pd.concat(results.values())
top = all_df.sort_values('r2 mean', ascending=False).head(10)
print(top[['model','dimension_reduction_type','r2 mean','r2 std','spearman_r mean','within_1_acc mean']].to_string(index=False))
"

Need to recompute ElasticNetCV properly — the auto-tuned `alpha`/`l1_ratio` differ per fold so groupby is creating per-fold rows. Let me aggregate by reduction type only:  

Ran terminal command: cd "c:\Users\Alecs\Documents\FMI 2025-2027\an1\sem2\BigData\Proiect-eda"; .venv\Scripts\python -c "
import pandas as pd

df = pd.read_csv('results/elasticnet_model.csv')
print('Columns:', df.columns.tolist())
print()
print(df.head(3).to_string())
print()
# Aggregate only by reduction type (ignore alpha/l1_ratio which vary per fold)
agg = df.groupby('dimension_reduction_type')[['r2','spearman_r','within_1_acc','mae','rmse']].agg(['mean','std']).reset_index()
agg.columns = ['dimension_reduction_type'] + [f'{m}_{s}' for m,s in agg.columns[1:]]
print('CV-averaged ElasticNetCV results:')
print(agg[['dimension_reduction_type','r2_mean','r2_std','spearman_r_mean','within_1_acc_mean','mae_mean']].to_string(index=False))
"

Completed: *Step 11: ElasticNet reduction integration* (4/5)

All 6 improvements are complete. Here's the summary:

**Steps 8–12 done. Final state:**

| Step | Best R² (5-fold CV) | Best combination |
|---|---|---|
| 8 – 5-fold CV | 0.518±0.057 | Linear + PCA n=50 |
| 9 – SVR(rbf) | 0.455±0.075 | SVR + PCA n=50 |
| 10 – SMOTE | 0.514±0.064 | Linear + PCA + SMOTE |
| **11/12 – ElasticNetCV** | **0.524±0.065** | **ElasticNetCV + PCA + SMOTE** |

**Notable finding from the logs:** ElasticNet with `alpha=1.0, l1_ratio=0.5` on 19,962 genes only produces **13–21 non-zero coefficients** — nearly as sparse as Lasso. The `top-200` selection fills the remainder with zero-coefficient features (noise). If you want ElasticNet to actually select 200 meaningful features you'd need to drop `alpha` to ~0.01–0.05. The ElasticNetCV *regressor* on the PCA space works well precisely because it auto-selects `l1_ratio=0.1` (near-Ridge), effectively acting as a tuned ridge regressor on the orthogonal PCA components.

Archives saved in: step8_5fold_cv, step9_svr, step10_smote, step11_12_elasticnet. big_eda_status.md updated with all sections including a final summary table.

Ran terminal command: cd "c:\Users\Alecs\Documents\FMI 2025-2027\an1\sem2\BigData\Proiect-eda"; .venv\Scripts\python -c "
import sys; sys.path.insert(0, '.')
import re, os
results_dir = 'results'
all_nums = []
for name in os.listdir(results_dir):
    m = re.match(r'step(\d+)', name)
    if m:
        all_nums.append(int(m.group(1)))
print('nums found:', sorted(all_nums))
print('next step:', max(all_nums) + 1 if all_nums else 1)
"

Next step will correctly be **14**. Everything is clean:

**What happened:** `step1_k100`, `step2_pca50`, `step5_log2_reg_metrics` etc. all had extra digits in their suffixes ("100", "50", "2") that inflated the max to 100 → step101. Fixed by matching only the leading digit group with `r"step(\d+)"`.

**Summary of this run (Step 13 – CV5, no SMOTE):**

| Best per model | R² mean±std | vs Step 11/12 (SMOTE) |
|---|---|---|
| ElasticNetCV | **0.511±0.073** (PCA n=50) | was 0.524±0.065 |
| Linear Model | **0.518±0.062** (PCA n=50) | — |
| Gradient Boosting | 0.447±0.073 | — |

Key finding: Removing SMOTE costs ElasticNetCV only ~0.013 R² (0.524 → 0.511), confirming SMOTE gave a small but real benefit. The MD section `## Step 13 – Cv5 No Smote` is now appended to big_eda_status.md with the full table, and the archive is at step13_cv5_no_smote.

import pandas as pd, os, glob

archive = r'c:\Users\Alecs\Documents\FMI 2025-2027\an1\sem2\BigData\Proiect-eda\results\step14_cv5_smote_vt0p10'
rows = []
for f in glob.glob(os.path.join(archive, '*.csv')):
    df = pd.read_csv(f)
    if 'r2' in df.columns and 'dimension_reduction_type' in df.columns:
        best = df.groupby('dimension_reduction_type')['r2'].mean().idxmax()
        best_r2 = df.groupby('dimension_reduction_type')['r2'].mean().max()
        best_std = df[df['dimension_reduction_type']==best]['r2'].std()
        spear = df[df['dimension_reduction_type']==best]['spearman_r'].mean() if 'spearman_r' in df.columns else float('nan')
        w1 = df[df['dimension_reduction_type']==best]['within_1_acc'].mean() if 'within_1_acc' in df.columns else float('nan')
        mae = df[df['dimension_reduction_type']==best]['mae'].mean() if 'mae' in df.columns else float('nan')
        model = os.path.basename(f).replace('.csv','')
        rows.append({'Model': model, 'Best Reduction': best, 'R2': f'{best_r2:.3f}±{best_std:.3f}', 'Spearman': f'{spear:.3f}', 'Within±1': f'{w1:.3f}', 'MAE': f'{mae:.3f}'})

rows.sort(key=lambda x: float(x['R2'].split('±')[0]), reverse=True)
for r in rows:
    print(f"{r['Model']:<22} {r['Best Reduction']:<20} R²={r['R2']}  ρ={r['Spearman']}  W±1={r['Within±1']}  MAE={r['MAE']}")
"

import pandas as pd, os, glob

archive = r'results\step14_cv5_smote_vt0p10'
rows = []
for f in glob.glob(os.path.join(archive, '*.csv')):
    df = pd.read_csv(f)
    if 'r2' in df.columns and 'dimension_reduction_type' in df.columns:
        grp = df.groupby('dimension_reduction_type')['r2'].mean()
        best = grp.idxmax()
        best_r2 = grp.max()
        best_std = df[df['dimension_reduction_type']==best]['r2'].std()
        spear = df[df['dimension_reduction_type']==best]['spearman_r'].mean() if 'spearman_r' in df.columns else float('nan')
        w1 = df[df['dimension_reduction_type']==best]['within_1_acc'].mean() if 'within_1_acc' in df.columns else float('nan')
        mae_v = df[df['dimension_reduction_type']==best]['mae'].mean() if 'mae' in df.columns else float('nan')
        model = os.path.basename(f).replace('.csv','')
        rows.append((best_r2, model, best, best_r2, best_std, spear, w1, mae_v))

rows.sort(reverse=True)
for _, model, best, r2, std, spear, w1, mae_v in rows:
    print(f'{model:<22} {best:<22} R2={r2:.3f}+-{std:.3f}  rho={spear:.3f}  W1={w1:.3f}  MAE={mae_v:.3f}')
"

**Step 14 vs Step 13 comparison** (SMOTE + variance filter vs no-SMOTE, no filter):

| Model | Step 13 R² | Step 14 R² | Δ | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| linear_model | 0.518±0.062 | **0.520±0.067** | +0.002 | marginal |
| elasticnet_model | 0.511±0.073 | **0.520±0.065** | +0.009 | tighter std |
| gradient_boosting | 0.447±0.073 | **0.464±0.043** | **+0.017** | much lower variance |
| random_forest | 0.440±0.082 | **0.456±0.052** | **+0.016** | much lower variance |
| svr_model | 0.370±0.114 | **0.401±0.086** | **+0.031** | biggest absolute gain |
| poisson_model | 0.360±0.131 | **0.367±0.130** | +0.007 | stable |
| decision_tree | 0.037±0.177 | -0.006±0.142 | −0.043 | worse, unstable |

Key takeaways:
- **Variance filter + SMOTE** helped ensemble methods (GBM, RF, SVR) most — both R² gain and reduced std
- Best reductions shifted: GBM and RF moved from PCA→ to **ElasticNet(top-200)** as best
- Decision tree continues to struggle regardless of preprocessing
